On the Composition of Drugs by Kind

De compositione medicamentorum per genera · Kühn XIII 362-1058

The first complete English translation we know of. No complete translation in any modern language. Parts exist in English: G. Baker 1574 (book 3, from a French epitome), M. Marai 2023 (substantial excerpts, Warwick PhD thesis) and ten reference passages (galen_project, 2026). This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks.

Greek (Kühn)

δεύτερον δὲ γεγυμνάσθαι κατὰ τὴν 459 ὁδὸν ταύτην ἐπὶ πολλοῖς τῶν κατὰ μέρος, εἰδότας ὅτι χωρὶς τοῦ γυμνάσασθαι τὸ τοιοῦτο γυμνάσιον οὐδὲν τῶν ἔργων ὀρθῶς περαίνεται. δεινὸν δὲ καὶ τὸ συμβὰν ἤδη τισὶν ὧν ἐγὼ γινώσκω δυσὶν ἰατροῖν. ὁ μὲν γὰρ ὑπὸ λύπης ἐπὶ τῇ τῶν τοιούτων γραφῶν ἀπωλείᾳ συντακεὶς ἀπέθανεν, ὁ δὲ ἀπέστη τῶν ἰατρικῶν ἔργων. ὑπάρχει δὲ τοῖς γυμνασθεῖσιν, ὡς ἐγὼ λέγω, πρὸς τῷ δύνασθαι συντιθέναι φάρμακα χρήσιμα καὶ τὸ κριτικοῖς εἶναι τῶν γεγραμμένων τοῖς πρὸ ἡμῶν ἰατροῖς, ἐπιτηδείως τε χρῆσθαι πᾶσι τοῖς ὑπ’ ἐκείνων γεγραμμένοις, οἷς τ’ ἂν αὐτὸς εὕρῃ κατὰ τὴν διδαχθησομένην μέθοδον. βέλτιον μὲν οὖν ἐστι τοῖς διὰ τῆς πείρας ἤδη βεβασανισμένοις χρῆσθαι, μαθόντα τῆς χρήσεως αὐτῶν τὴν μέθοδον, ἀλλ’ ὅπερ ἔφην, ἀνάγκη καταλαμβάνει πολλάκις ἡμᾶς, ὡς καὶ τοῖς ἀπειράστοις αὐτοσχεδιάζοντας χρῆσθαι. κατὰ μὲν οὖν τὸ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίον, ὅπως ἄν τις ἐκ λιθαργύρου μόνης ἐμπλάστρους σκευάζοι, λέλεκται ποτὲ μὲν ἔλαιον μόνον μιγνὺς, ποτὲ δ’ ὄξος ἢ οἶνον ἤ τινα ταύτων, ἐξ ὁποίας τε συμμετρίας αὐτῶν ἔμπλαστρος δύναται γίγνεσθαι λευκὴ προσηκόντως ἑψηθεῖσα.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤ τινα ταύτων

and second, to have been trained along this 459 path in many of the particular cases, knowing that without having trained oneself in such an exercise none of the works is accomplished correctly. Terrible too is what has already befallen certain persons whom I know, two physicians: for the one, wasted away by grief at the loss of such writings, died, while the other withdrew from medical practice. But those who have been trained, as I say, possess, in addition to being able to compose useful drugs, also the capacity to be discriminating judges of what has been written by the physicians before us, and to make suitable use both of everything written by them and of whatever one may oneself discover according to the method that is going to be taught. Now it is better to use the drugs already tested through experience, having learned the method of their use; but, as I said, necessity often overtakes us, so that we must use even untried ones, improvising. In the book before this one, then, it has been stated how one may prepare plasters from litharge alone, sometimes mixing in oil only, sometimes vinegar or wine or some of these together, and from what proportion of them a white plaster can be produced when properly boiled.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ταύτων (read as τούτων, 'some of these'; possibly ταὐτῶν), τὸ τοιοῦτο γυμνάσιον (cognate accusative with γυμνάσασθαι, rendered as 'such training')

secondly, to have been trained in this 459 way on many particular cases, knowing that without having gone through such training none of the tasks is carried out correctly. Terrible, too, is what has already befallen certain people, two physicians whom I know. For the one, wasting away from grief at the loss of such writings, died, and the other withdrew from medical practice. Those who have been trained in the way I describe have, besides being able to compound useful drugs, also the capacity to be critical judges of what was written by the physicians before us, and to make suitable use both of everything written by them and of whatever one discovers oneself according to the method that is going to be taught. It is better, then, to use those already tested through experience, once one has learned the method of their use; but, as I said, necessity often overtakes us, so that we also use untried ones, improvising. In the book before this one, then, it has been stated how one might prepare plasters from litharge alone, mixing in sometimes oil alone, and sometimes vinegar or wine or some of these, and from what proportion of them a white plaster can be produced when properly boiled.

Greek (Kühn)

γίγνεσθαι δ’ 460 ἔφαμεν ἐκ τούτων τῶν ἐμπλάστρων ἐνίας μὲν ἐπουλωτικὰς, ἐνίας δὲ καὶ κολλητικὰς ἐναίμων τραυμάτων, ἐνίας δὲ φλεγμονῶν τῶν μικρῶν θεραπευτικὰς, ἐνίας δὲ αὐτῶν καὶ τὰ δυσεπούλωτα θεραπεύειν, ὁπόσα μὴ κακοήθη καί τινας ἐξ αὐτῶν ὄγκους παρὰ φύσιν, ὅσοι μὴ μεγάλοι μηδὲ σκιῤῥώδεις εἰσὶ διαφορεῖν. ἐγράφη δ’ οὐ μετὰ πολὺ τῆς ἀρχῆς ἐν αὐτῷ καὶ ἡ ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ συντεθεῖσα ἔμπλαστρος, ἣν ὀνομάζω φοινικίνην. ἐν τούτῳ δὲ τῷ βιβλίῳ τῷ νῦν ἡμῖν προκειμένῳ περί τε τῶν χλωρῶν ἐμπλάστρων εἰρήσεται καὶ μηλίνων καὶ κιῤῥῶν καὶ φαιῶν καὶ πυῤῥῶν, ἐν αἷς εἰσι καὶ αἱ δίχρωμοι καλούμεναι, μεταξύ τε τούτων ἕτεραι τινὲς μὲν ὠνομασμέναι, καθάπερ λευκομήλιναί τε καὶ μελάγχλωροι καὶ ῥυπώδεις ἀερίζουσαί τε καὶ κυανίζουσαι, τινὲς δὲ καὶ παντάπασιν ἀνώνυμοι. τὸ μὲν οὖν ἐφεξῆς ἀλλήλαις γράφειν τὰς κατὰ δύναμιν ὁμοίας χρήσιμόν ἐστιν εἰς τὸ πλείοσιν ἐντυγχάνοντα ἢ ἀναγινώσκοντα τὸ βιβλίον ἑλέσθαι τὴν ἐπιτηδειοτάτην τῷ θεραπευομένῳ σώματί τε καὶ πάθει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥυπώδεις, ἀερίζουσαι, κυανίζουσαι, πλείοσιν ἐντυγχάνοντα

And we said that of these plasters 460 some turn out to be cicatrizing, some also agglutinative of bloody wounds, some curative of small inflammations, and that some of them also treat the ulcers that are hard to cicatrize, so far as these are not malignant, and that some of them disperse tumours contrary to nature, so far as these are neither large nor scirrhous. There was also written in it, not long after the beginning, the plaster compounded by me, which I call phoinikine. In this book, the one now before us, there will be discussion of the green plasters and the quince-yellow and the tawny and the grey and the reddish ones, among which are also those called two-coloured, and between these others, some of them bearing names, such as the whitish-quince-yellow and the blackish-green and the dirty-coloured and the sky-blue-tinged and the dark-blue-tinged, while some are altogether nameless. Now, to write one after another those that are similar in power is useful for enabling one who meets with several of them, or who reads the book, to choose the one most suitable for the body and the affection that is being treated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀερίζουσαι, κυανίζουσαι, ῥυπώδεις, πυῤῥῶν, φαιῶν, πλείοσιν ἐντυγχάνοντα

We said 460 that from these plasters there come some that are cicatrising, some also that are agglutinative of bloody wounds, some that are curative of small inflammations, some of them that also treat sores hard to cicatrise, as many as are not malignant, and some of them that disperse swellings contrary to nature, as many as are neither large nor scirrhous. And not long after its beginning there was also written in it the plaster composed by me, which I call the palm plaster (phoinikinē). In this book, the one now before us, there will be discussion of the green plasters, and of the quince-yellow, the tawny, the dusky and the flame-red ones, among which are also the so-called two-coloured plasters, and, in between these, certain others: some that have names, such as the whitish quince-yellow, the dark green, the dirty-coloured, and those tending to the colour of the air and to dark blue, and some that are altogether nameless. Now, to write down next to one another those that are similar in power is useful, so that the one who comes upon several of them, or who reads the book, may choose the plaster most suitable to the body under treatment and to the affection.

K13.460-461K. XIII 460–461, ch. 2.1 difficulty high (6.85/100; thin 9, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 7/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

τὸ δὲ τὰς κατὰ χρόαν ὁμοίας ἀναγράφειν, οὐ κατὰ τεχνικὴν ὁμοιότητα 461 σκοπούντων ἐστὶν, ἀλλ’ ἐπεί τινες ἐνίοτε τῶν πολὺ δυναμένων ἀνδρῶν κελεύουσιν ἡμᾶς ἔμπλαστρον αὐτοῖς μηλίνην ποιῆσαι κολλητικὴν ἑλκῶν ἢ ἐπουλωτικὴν ἢ μαλακτικὴν τῶν σκληρυνομένων ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἐργάσασθαι δυναμένην, ἠναγκάσθησαν ἔνιοι τῶν νεωτέρων ἰατρῶν ἐφεξῆς ἀλλήλων γράφοντες ὁμοίας κατὰ χρῶμα προσγράφειν ἑκάστῃ τὰς οἰκείας ἐπαγγελίας. ἐγὼ τοίνυν πειράσομαι μικτήν τινα ποιήσασθαι τὴν διδασκαλίαν ἀπὸ τῆς τεχνικωτέρας ἀρξάμενος. γράφω γὰρ πρῶτον μὲν τὰς σαρκωτικὰς τῶν κοίλων ἑλκῶν, ὧν αἱ πλεῖσται χλωραὶ κατὰ χρόαν εἰσὶν, ἐπισημαινόμενος ὅσαι τε κολλητικαὶ τούτων ὑπάρχουσι καὶ ὅσαι τῶν κακοηθευομένων ἰατρικαὶ καί τι τῶν τοιούτων ἄλλο δυνάμεναι διαπράττεσθαι. δευτέρας δὲ τὰς κολλητικὰς, οὐ μόνον τῶν προσφάτων ἑλκῶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν κόλπων, ἐν αἷς εἰσι καὶ μέχρι συνουλώσεως ἄγουσαι πολλαί. καὶ τούτων ἁπασῶν ἕνα μὲν στίχον ποιήσομαι τῶν μηλίνων, ἕτερον δὲ τῶν βαρβάρων ὀνομαζομένων, ἕτερον δὲ φαιῶν, ἐπί τε τῶν ἄλλων χρωμάτων ὁμοίως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But to record those that are alike in colour is not the practice of people who look to likeness according to the art; 461 rather, since some of the very powerful men sometimes order us to make for them a quince-yellow plaster that is agglutinative of wounds, or cicatrizing, or emollient of parts that are becoming hardened, or able to effect something of this sort, some of the more recent physicians have been compelled, in writing one after another those that are alike in colour, to add to each its proper professed effects. I, then, shall try to make my instruction a mixed one, beginning from the more technical arrangement. For I write first the flesh-producing plasters for hollow ulcers, most of which are green in colour, indicating which of these are agglutinative as well, and which are curative of ulcers that are turning malignant, and which are able to accomplish something else of this kind. Second, the agglutinative ones, not only for recent wounds but also for sinuses, among which there are also many that lead right up to cicatrization. And of all these I shall make one series of the quince-yellow ones, another of those called barbarian, another of the grey ones, and likewise for the other colours.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μηλίνην, φαιῶν, βαρβάρων, στίχον, ἐπαγγελίας, κατὰ τεχνικὴν ὁμοιότητα σκοπούντων

But recording together those that are similar in colour is not the practice of people who look to a technical similarity 461. However, some men of great power sometimes order us to make them a quince-yellow plaster that is agglutinative of wounds, or cicatrizing, or emollient of parts that are becoming hardened, or able to accomplish something else of that kind. For that reason some of the more recent physicians were compelled to write those similar in colour one after another and to add to each its proper claims. I shall therefore try to make my teaching a kind of mixture, beginning from the more technical arrangement. For first I write the flesh-producing plasters for hollow wounds, most of which are green in colour, noting which of them are also agglutinative, which are curative of wounds turning malignant, and which can accomplish something else of that kind. Second, I write the agglutinative ones, not only of fresh wounds but also of sinuses, among which there are also many that carry the healing through to cicatrisation. And of all these I shall make one row of the quince-yellow ones, another of those called 'barbarian', another of the grey ones, and likewise for the other colours.

K13.461-462K. XIII 461–462, ch. 2.1 difficulty mid (4.44/100; thin 2, absent 4) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) judge_failed_gate unit: pass

Greek (Kühn)

ἐρῶ δὲ καὶ καθ’ ἓν ἄλλο βιβλίον 462 ἄνευ χρωμάτων ὁμοιότητας, ὅσαι τὰ δυσεπούλωτα τῶν ἑλκῶν θεραπεύουσιν, ἃ καὶ χειρώνεια καλοῦσι. καὶ μετὰ τοῦτο καθ’ ἕτερον βιβλίον, ὅσαι καὶ ταῦτα καὶ ἄλλ’ ἄττα πολύχρηστοι λεγόμεναί τε καὶ οὖσαι. καὶ μετὰ ταύτας ἁπάσας ὅσον ὑπόλοιπόν ἐστι τῶν κατὰ τὰς δυνάμεις ἐμπλάστρων ἐγνῶσθαι μεθόδῳ διελθεῖν πειράσομαι. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν προείρηται κοινόν τι προοίμιον ἁπασῶν τῶν προσγραφησομένων ἐμπλάστρων, ἐπὶ δὲ τὰς ἐν τούτῳ τῷ βιβλίῳ γεγραμμένας μεταβήσομαι, προτάξας αὐτῶν τινα λόγον κοινὸν, ὡς εἰ καὶ μηδὲν ἤδη μοι προείρητο. φαρμάκων συνθέσεις παμπόλλων ἀξίων ἐπαίνου πρῶτος ὧν οἶδα Μαντείας ὁ Ἡροφίλειος ἔγραψεν, οὗ μαθητὴς γενόμενος Ἡρακλείδης ὁ Ταραντῖνος οὐ μόνον ἐν τῇ τῶν φαρμάκων χρήσει φαίνεται μιμούμενος ἐκεῖνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ κατὰ τὸ διαιτητικὸν μέρος τῆς τέχνης. μεθ’ οὓς ἄλλοι μέν τινες ἐν πλείοσι βιβλίοις, ὥσπερ ὅ τε Μαντείας καὶ Ἡρακλείδης.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And I shall also state, in one other book 462 without similarities of colour, all those that treat the wounds that are hard to cicatrize, which they also call Chironian. And after this, in yet another book, all those that treat both these and certain other things, being both called and actually being polychrēsta (of many uses). And after all these I shall try to go through by method whatever remains to be known of the plasters according to their powers. These things, then, have been said beforehand as a kind of common preface to all the plasters that are going to be written up hereafter; and I shall pass over to those written in this book, having set before them a certain common account, as if nothing had already been said beforehand by me. Compositions of a very great many drugs worthy of praise were written, first of those I know, by Mantias the Herophilean, whose pupil Heraclides of Tarentum appears to imitate him not only in the use of drugs but also in the dietetic part of the art. After whom certain others (wrote) in several books, just as both Mantias and Heraclides did.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἄνευ χρωμάτων ὁμοιότητας (accusative plural after ἄνευ is anomalous; perhaps read ὁμοιότητος), ἐγνῶσθαι μεθόδῳ (construction of the perfect infinitive unclear), χειρώνεια, μεθ’ οὓς ἄλλοι μέν τινες ἐν πλείοσι βιβλίοις (verb understood)

I shall also speak, in one further book 462, without regard to similarities of colour, of those that treat ulcers which are difficult to cicatrise, which they also call Chironian. And after this, in another book, of those that treat both these and certain other conditions, which are called polychrest and are so in fact. And after all these I shall attempt to go through by method whatever remains to be known of the plasters classified according to their faculties. These things, then, have been said beforehand as a kind of common preface to all the plasters that are to be recorded; but I shall now pass on to those written in this book, placing before them a common account, as if nothing had already been said by me beforehand. The first, of those I know, to write down the compositions of very many drugs worthy of praise was Mantias the Herophilean; Heracleides of Tarentum, who became his pupil, is seen to imitate him not only in the use of drugs but also in the dietetic part of the art. After them certain others wrote in several books, just as both Mantias and Heracleides had done.

εἰσὶ δὲ καὶ οἱ καθ’ ἓν ἢ δύο μόνα τὴν γραφὴν αὐτῶν ἐποιήσαντο, καθάπερ ὁ Καππαδόκης Ἥρας, οὔτ’ ἀδόκιμον γράψας τι φάρμακον, οὔτε ﻿ 463 τὰς σκευασίας αὐτῶν καταλιπὼν τάς τε κατὰ μέρος χρήσεις ἁπάσας διελθών. πολλὰ δὲ φάρμακα γεγράφασιν ἐπιμελῶς ἐν πλείοσι βιβλίοις ὅ τε Μούσας καὶ ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης καὶ ὁ Κρίτων. ὁ δ’ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἐν ἑνὶ μὲν βιβλίῳ τὰ τῶν ἐκτὸς τοῦ σώματος ἰατρικὰ φάρμακα, καθ’ ἕτερον δὲ ἓν ἔγραψε τὰ τῶν ἐντὸς, ὄντος καὶ τρίτου μικροτέρου τούτων ὀφθαλμικοῦ. οὐ πρόσκειται δὲ ὀλιγίστων αὐτῶν ἥ τε σκευασία καὶ ἡ χρῆσις. οὔτε δὲ τούτων τις οὔτε ἄλλος ἔγραψε τὸν λογισμὸν τῆς συνθέσεως ἑκάστου τῶν φαρμάκων, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο θαῤῥαλεώτερον οἱ ἀπὸ τῆς ἐμπειρίας ἀποφαίνονται, καίτοι γε ἀδιανόητα λέγοντες, εἰς περίπτωσιν γὰρ ἀναφέρουσι τὴν εὕρεσιν τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων. ἐθάῤῥησαν δὲ τῷ μηδένα τῶν δογματικῶν ἰατρῶν διηρθρωμένως γεγραφέναι τὴν μέθοδον τῆς συνθέσεως αὐτῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὐ πρόσκειται δὲ ὀλιγίστων αὐτῶν

There are also those who made their record of them in only one or two books, as Heras the Cappadocian did, who neither wrote down any drug that is not approved, nor 463 left out their preparations, and went through all their particular uses. Musa, Asclepiades and Criton have written up many drugs carefully in several books. Andromachus wrote in one book the medical drugs for the parts outside the body, and in another single one those for the parts within, there being also a third, smaller than these, on eye-remedies. For very few of them, however, is the preparation and the use not added. But neither any of these men nor anyone else wrote the reasoning behind the composition of each of the drugs, and for this reason those of the empirical school make their pronouncements with greater boldness, even though what they say is inconceivable, for they refer the discovery of compound drugs to chance encounter. They have taken courage from the fact that none of the dogmatic physicians has written out in articulated form the method of their composition.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὐ πρόσκειται δὲ ὀλιγίστων, ἀδόκιμον

There are also those who set down their account of them in one or two books only, as Heras of Cappadocia did, who neither recorded any untested drug nor 463 left out their preparations, and went through all their particular uses. Many drugs have been written up carefully, in several books, by Musa, by Asclepiades and by Crito. Andromachus wrote the curative drugs for the parts outside the body in one book, and those for the parts inside in another single book, and there is also a third, smaller than these, on eye remedies. For a very few of them the preparation and the use are not appended. But neither any of these nor anyone else wrote out the reasoning behind the composition of each of the drugs, and for this reason those from experience [the Empiricists] make their pronouncements more boldly, although what they say is unintelligible, for they refer the discovery of compound drugs to accidental encounter (periptōsis). They took courage from the fact that none of the Dogmatic physicians had written out in an articulated way the method of their composition.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅταν γὰρ οἱ δογματίζοντες ἐρωτώμενοι τὸν λόγον, ᾧ σύγκειται τῶν φαρμάκων ἕκαστον οἷς χρῶνται, μηδὲν ἔχωσι λέγειν, ἀπολέσαντές τε τὰς γραφὰς αὐτῶν ἀδυνατῶσιν αὐτοσχεδιάζειν ἑτέρας συνθέσεις, αὐτοί τε εἰκότως καταφρονοῦνται, τούς τε ἐμπειρικοὺς ἐργάζονται 464 τολμηροτέρους εἰς ἀντιλογίαν. εἰ δ’ ἠπίσταντο τὴν ἑκάστου πάθους ὧν θεραπεύουσι φύσιν ἐγίγνωσκόν τε μέθοδον, ὡς ἐξ αὐτῆς ἐξευρίσκειν τόν τε σκοπὸν τῆς θεραπείας, ἑκάστου τε τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων τὴν δύναμιν, οὐκ ἂν ἠγνόουν τὸν λόγον τῆς συνθέσεως· οἷον αὐτίκα κοῖλον ἕλκος ἀπερίστατον, οὐδὲν γὰρ χεῖρον, ὡς ἔνιοι καλοῦσι, συντόμου διδασκαλίας ἕνεκεν οὕτως καὶ ἡμᾶς ὀνομάσαι, πῶς ἄν τις ἰάσαιτο προκείσθω σκοπεῖν, ὡς εἰ καὶ μήπω μηδὲν εὕροιτο φάρμακον ἐπιτήδειον εἰς αὐτό. διδαχθέντες γὰρ ὑπὸ τοῦ λόγου τὴν τῶν κοίλων ἑλκῶν ἴασιν γίγνεσθαι γιγνομένης σαρκὸς ἀντὶ τῆς ἀπολωλυίας ἑτέρας, ἐξ αὐτῆς δηλονότι βλαστανούσης τῆς ὑποκειμένης σαρκὸς, ἐνενοήσαμεν ἀδύνατον εἶναι γενέσθαι τοῦτο, ῥύπου κατὰ τὸ ἕλκος ἢ πολλῆς ὑγρότητος ὑποκειμένης. ἀλλὰ τὸν μὲν ῥύπον ἀφαιρεῖ τὰ ῥυπτικὰ τῶν φαρμάκων, τὴν δ’ ὑγρότητα διαφορεῖ τὰ ξηραντικά.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀπερίστατον

For when those who dogmatize, on being asked the rationale by which each of the drugs they use is composed, have nothing to say, and, if they have lost their written recipes for them, are unable to improvise other compositions, they are themselves deservedly despised, and they make the Empiricists 464 bolder in contradiction. But if they understood the nature of each affection among those they treat, and knew a method such that from it they could discover both the aim of the treatment and the power of each of the simple drugs, they would not be ignorant of the rationale of the composition. For example, to begin with, let it be set before us to consider how one might heal a hollow uncomplicated ulcer (for it is no worse that we too should name it thus, as some call it, for the sake of concise teaching), as if no drug suitable for it had yet been discovered. For having been taught by reason that the healing of hollow ulcers comes about when other flesh is generated in place of that which has been lost, sprouting evidently from the underlying flesh itself, we realized that it is impossible for this to happen when filth or much moisture lies in the ulcer. But the detergent drugs remove the filth, while the drying ones disperse the moisture.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπερίστατον, ὑποκειμένης, ῥυπτικὰ, ἐγίγνωσκόν τε μέθοδον, ὡς ἐξ αὐτῆς ἐξευρίσκειν τόν τε σκοπὸν τῆς θεραπείας, ἑκάστου τε τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων τὴν δύναμιν

For when the dogmatists, asked for the reasoning by which each of the drugs they use is compounded, have nothing to say, and, having lost their written formulas, are unable to improvise other compoundings, they are themselves rightly despised, and they make the Empiricists 464 bolder in contradiction. But if they understood the nature of each affection that they treat, and knew a method such that from it they could discover both the aim of the treatment and the power of each of the simple drugs, they would not be ignorant of the reasoning of compounding. For instance, let it be proposed to consider how one would cure a hollow ulcer that is uncomplicated (for there is no harm in our also naming it so, as some call it, for the sake of concise teaching), as though no drug suitable for it had yet been found. For, having been taught by reason that the healing of hollow ulcers comes about when other flesh is generated in place of that which has been destroyed, sprouting evidently from the underlying flesh itself, we have realised that this cannot come about while there is filth in the ulcer or much moisture present. But the detergent drugs remove the filth, and the drying ones disperse the moisture.

Greek (Kühn)

χρὴ τοίνυν ἐπίστασθαι τὰς ὕλας τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων, οὐχ ἁπλῶς κατὰ γένος, ὡς Διοσκουρίδης ἄλλως ἔγραψεν, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἡμεῖς ἐποιήσαμεν ἐν ταῖς περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων, 465 οὐ μόνον γράψαντες ἤτοι θερμαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον ἐργάζεσθαι τόδε τι τὸ φάρμακον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅτι κατὰ τὴν πρώτην ἀπόστασιν ἢ δευτέραν ἢ τρίτην ἢ τετάρτην τοῦ μέσου φάρμακον τοιοῦτον εἴη. καὶ πολλάκις γε ὅτι κατὰ μεσοῦσαν ἑκάστην τούτων ἢ ἀρχομένην ἢ τελευτῶσαν, ὡς εἶναι δώδεκα τάξεις τῶν κατ’ ἐπικράτειαν ἡντιναοῦν λεγομένων φαρμάκων. ἐνίοτε μὲν γὰρ ἤτοι μετρίως θερμαίνοντος ἢ ξηραίνοντος, ἐνίοτε δὲ σφοδρότατα ποιεῖν ὁτιοῦν τούτων δυναμένου χρῄζομεν. ὁ δ’ ἁπλῶς ὅτι θερμανθῆναι δεῖται τὸ μόριον ἐπιστάμενος οὐδὲν μᾶλλον αἱρήσεται λιβανωτοῦ πέπερι καὶ νᾶπυ. ταῦτ’ οὖν εἴρηται μὲν ἡμῖν καὶ πρόσθεν, εἴρηται δὲ κᾀν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου. ὡς προεπισταμένους δ’ αὐτὰ μετ’ ἀποδείξεως τῆς προσηκούσης, ἐπὶ τὰ λοιπὰ προάξω τὸν ἐνεστῶτα λόγον ἐντεῦθεν ἀρξάμενος.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄλλως, ἀπόστασιν

It is necessary, then, to know the materials of such drugs, not simply by kind, as Dioscorides wrote in a different manner, but as we did in the books on simple drugs, 465 writing not only that this particular drug heats or cools or produces some other such effect, but also that the drug is of such a kind at the first remove from the mean, or the second or the third or the fourth; and often, indeed, that it is at the middle of each of these, or at its beginning or its end, so that there are twelve orders of the drugs that are spoken of according to any predominance whatsoever. For sometimes we need one that heats or dries moderately, and sometimes one that is able to do any of these things most vehemently. But he who knows simply that the part needs to be heated will no more choose pepper and mustard than frankincense. These things, then, have been said by us before, and have been said also in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. Taking it that they are already known together with the appropriate demonstration, I shall lead the present discourse forward to what remains, beginning from here.

It is necessary, then, to know the materials of such drugs, not simply by kind, as Dioscorides wrote in another way, but as we did in our works on simple drugs, 465 where we wrote not only that this particular drug heats or cools or produces some other such effect, but also that it is a drug of that kind in the first remove from the mean, or the second, or the third, or the fourth. And often, too, we wrote that it is so at the middle of each of these, or at its beginning, or at its end, so that there are twelve ranks of the drugs named according to any predominance whatever. These things have been said by us before, and they have also been said in the third book of the Therapeutic Method. But since my readers already know them, together with the appropriate demonstration, I shall carry the present discussion forward to the remaining matters, beginning from this point.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπειδὴ ξηραίνοντός τε καὶ ῥύπτοντος μετρίως ἄνευ δήξεως ἐδείχθη δεόμενα τὰ σαρκωθησόμενα τῶν ἑλκῶν, ἀναμνησθῆναι χρὴ τὴν ὕλην τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων 466 ἥτις ἐστί. δέδεικται δὲ ἐν τῇ περὶ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως πραγματείᾳ, μετὰ τοῦ καὶ τὴν προσήκουσαν ἑκάστου τάξιν εἰρῆσθαι. τοῦτο γὰρ, ὡς ἔφην, ἔστι τὸ μάλιστα χρήσιμον, ὥστε ἐάν ποτε δέηταί τις ἰσχυροτέρου φαρμάκου, γινώσκειν ἐφ’ ὃ μεταβήσεται, μὴ παρόντος δὲ τῶν ἁπλῶν μηδενὸς, ἐπὶ τὸ σύνθετον ἐρχόμεθα. καὶ πρῶτόν γε ὑποκείσθω κηρὸν ἔχειν ἡμᾶς καὶ ἰὸν, ἄλλο δὲ μηδὲν τῶν ἁρμοττόντων ἄντικρυς ἕλκει κοίλῳ, λιβανωτὸν λέγω καὶ ἴριν καὶ ἀριστολοχίαν, ἄλευρόν τε τὸ τῶν ὀρόβων καὶ πάνακος ῥίζαν, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα. γιγνώσκοντες οὖν ὅτι κηροῦ μὲν ἐπιτεθέντος μόνου ῥυπαρὸν εὑρεθήσεται τὸ ἕλκος, ὡς ἂν μήτε ῥύπτειν μήτε ξηραίνειν δυναμένου, δηχθήσεται δὲ καὶ διαβρωθήσεται καὶ φλεγμανεῖ προσαχθέντος ἰοῦ. τούτων μὲν ἑκάτερον παραιτησόμεθα, μέσον δ’ ἐξ ἀμφοῖν ἄλλο ποιήσομεν, ἀσθενέστερον μὲν ἢ κατὰ τὸν ἰὸν, ἰσχυρότερον δὲ κηροῦ. ἀλλ’ ἐπεὶ βραχὺ μὲν ἀπολείπεται τοῦ χρησίμως ἂν προσαχθέντος ὁ κηρὸς, ὁ δ’ ἰὸς ὑπερβάλλει πολὺ, πολλῷ τῷ κηρῷ μίξομεν ὀλίγον ἰοῦ. πόσον οὖν πόσῳ;

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Since it has been shown that those ulcers which are to be filled with flesh require a drug that dries and cleanses moderately, without bitingness, one must recall what the material of such drugs 466 is. It has been shown in the treatise on the power of simple drugs, together with the appropriate rank of each also having been stated. For this, as I said, is the most useful thing: so that, if ever someone needs a stronger drug, he knows to which one he will pass over; but when none of the simples is at hand, we come to the compound. And first let it be supposed that we have wax and verdigris, but nothing else of the things directly suited to a hollow ulcer—I mean frankincense and iris and aristolochia, and the flour of bitter vetch and the root of panax, and whatever other such things there are. Knowing, then, that if wax alone is applied the ulcer will be found sordid, inasmuch as wax is able neither to cleanse nor to dry, whereas if verdigris is applied it will be bitten and eaten away and will become inflamed, we shall decline each of these, and shall make from both another, intermediate one, weaker than verdigris but stronger than wax. But since wax falls short only a little of what would be usefully applied, whereas verdigris exceeds it by much, we shall mix a little verdigris with much wax. How much, then, with how much?

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: μὴ παρόντος δὲ τῶν ἁπλῶν μηδενὸς, ἐπὶ τὸ σύνθετον ἐρχόμεθα

Since it was shown that ulcers which are to be filled with flesh need a drug that dries and cleanses moderately without biting, we must recall what the material of such drugs 466 is. It has been set out in the treatise On the Power of Simple Drugs, together with a statement of the rank appropriate to each. For this, as I said, is the most useful thing: that if ever one needs a stronger drug, one knows what to pass on to; and when none of the simples is at hand, we come to the compound. First, then, let it be supposed that we have wax and verdigris, but nothing else of the things directly suited to a hollow ulcer, by which I mean frankincense, iris and birthwort, meal of bitter vetch, root of panax, and all other such things. We know, then, that if wax alone is applied the ulcer will be found foul, since wax can neither cleanse nor dry, whereas if verdigris is applied the ulcer will be bitten and eroded and will become inflamed. We shall therefore decline each of these and make from both together another drug, intermediate between them, weaker than verdigris but stronger than wax. But since wax falls a little short of what could usefully be applied, while verdigris exceeds it by much, we shall mix a little verdigris with much wax. How much, then, to how much?

Greek (Kühn)

467 τοῦτο γὰρ ἐφεξῆς εἰπεῖν ἐστιν, ἀκριβῶς μὲν οὐχ οἷόν τε τὸ πόσον εὑρεῖν ἑκατέρου πρὸ τῆς πείρας, στοχάσασθαι δὲ οἷόν τε καὶ κρῖναι καὶ συνθεῖναι τὴν πρώτην, μηδεμίαν ἀποτυχίαν ἀξιόλογον ἕξον φάρμακον, ὥσπερ ἐγώ ποτ’ ἐποίησα κατά τινα κώμην, ἐν ᾗ μήτ’ αὐτὸς εἶχόν τι φάρμακον ἑπόμενον, ἄλλος τε οὐδ’ ηὐπόρει τῶν κατ’ αὐτὴν, εὗρόν τε μόνα τῶν ἐπιτηδείων εἰς σύνθεσιν ἰὸν καὶ κηρόν. ἀναλαβὼν δ’ ἀμφότερα καὶ τήξας ἐπὶ πυρὸς τὸν κηρὸν σὺν ἐλαίῳ ῥοδίνῳ, ὡς γενέσθαι κηρωτὴν ὑγρὰν, ἔμιξα τῇ λίτρᾳ τῆς κηρωτῆς οὐγγίαν μίαν ἰοῦ, τουτέστιν τὸ δωδέκατον μέρος. ἐνενόησα γὰρ ἢ τὸ δέκατον ἢ τὸ ιβ΄. μῖξαι τὸ πλέον μὲν ὑποπτεύσας, ὡς δριμύτερον ἐσόμενον τοῦ κηροῦ, τὸ δ’ ἔλαττον, ὡς ἀσθενέστερον. ἐλθόντες δὲ εἰς πεῖραν τοῦ συντεθέντος ἐφάνη καὶ τὸ δέκατον καὶ τὸ δωδέκατον ὀρθῶς μιγνύμενα κατὰ τὰς διαφορὰς τῶν σωμάτων. ὑπεδείχθη γὰρ ἡμῖν, ὅπερ καὶ διὰ τῆς πείρας μαρτυρεῖται, τὰ μὲν ἰσχυρὰ σώματα φαρμάκων ἰσχυροτέρων ἀνεχόμενα, τὰ δ’ ἀσθενέστερα μὴ φέροντα τὴν τῶν τοιούτων προσφοράν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δριμύτερον ἐσόμενον τοῦ κηροῦ

467 For this is the next thing to state: it is not possible to find exactly the quantity of each before trial, but it is possible to make a conjecture and to judge and to compound at the first attempt a drug that will have no failure worth mentioning, just as I myself once did in a certain village, in which I neither had any drug with me myself, nor was anyone else among the people there well supplied, and I found, of the things suitable for compounding, only verdigris and wax. Taking up both and melting the wax over a fire together with rose oil, so that a liquid cerate came about, I mixed into a litra of the cerate one ounce of verdigris, that is, the twelfth part. For I had in mind to mix in either the tenth or the twelfth part, suspecting the greater amount as likely to prove more pungent than the wax, and the lesser as likely to be weaker. When we came to a trial of what had been compounded, both the tenth and the twelfth proved to be correctly mixed according to the differences of bodies. For it was indicated to us—which is also attested through experience—that strong bodies tolerate stronger drugs, whereas weaker ones do not bear the application of such drugs.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἑπόμενον, ηὐπόρει, δριμύτερον ἐσόμενον τοῦ κηροῦ, τὴν πρώτην

467 For this is what must be stated next: that it is not possible to find out exactly the quantity of each before trial, but it is possible to make a conjecture, to judge, and to compose at the first attempt a drug that will have no failure worth mentioning. I once did just this in a certain village, where I myself had no drug accompanying me, nor was any other of the people there supplied with one, and I found, of the things suitable for a composition, only verdigris and wax. Taking both, and melting the wax over a fire together with rose-oil so that a liquid cerate was produced, I mixed with the pound of cerate one ounce of verdigris, that is, the twelfth part. For I had considered mixing either the tenth or the twelfth part, suspecting that the larger would be more acrid than the wax, and the smaller weaker. When we came to the trial of the compound, both the tenth and the twelfth appeared to be correctly mixed, according to the differences of bodies. For it was indicated to us, as is also attested through experience, that strong bodies tolerate stronger drugs, whereas the weaker ones do not bear the application of such drugs.

ἰσχυρότερα δὲ λέγω σώματα ﻿ 468 τὰ ξηρότερα ταῖς κράσεσιν, ὁποῖα τά τε τῶν γεωργῶν ἐστὶ καὶ ναυτῶν καὶ κυνηγετῶν. ἀσθενέστερα δὲ τὰ μαλακώτερα κατὰ φύσιν ἢ ἔθος, ὁποῖα τὰ τῶν γυναικῶν, εὐνούχων τε καὶ παίδων, ὅσοι τ’ εἰσὶν ὑγροὶ φύσει ταῖς κράσεσι λευκὸν καὶ μαλακὸν ἔχοντες τὸ σῶμα. τὰ μὲν οὖν σκληρὰ σώματα πλέονος ἰοῦ τῇ κηρωτῇ μιγνυμένου σαρκοῦται, τὰ μαλακὰ δὲ ἐλάττονος. ὅθεν, ὡς εἴρηται πολλάκις, ἓν φάρμακον ἀδύνατόν ἐστι πᾶσιν ἁρμόττειν σώμασιν, ἀλλὰ χρὴ μέσον μὲν εἶναι τὸ φάρμακον, ὡς πρὸς τὴν μέσην κρᾶσιν ἡρμοσμένον, ἄγεσθαι δ’ ἐφ’ ἑκάτερα τῇ καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν χρήσει κρινόμενον. ἐὰν μὲν γὰρ φαίνηται τὸ δέον εἰργασμένον, ἐπιμένειν αὐτῷ προσήκει. ἐὰν δ’ ἐλλιπέστερον, αὐξάνειν αὐτοῦ τὴν δύναμιν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ καθαιρεῖν, ἡνίκα περαιτέρω τοῦ προσήκοντος ἐνήργησεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

By stronger bodies I mean 468 those that are drier in their temperaments, such as are those of farmers, sailors and hunters; by weaker, those that are softer by nature or by habit, such as those of women, eunuchs and children, and all who are moist by nature in their temperaments, having a white and soft body. Hard bodies, then, are made to grow flesh when more verdigris is mixed with the cerate, soft bodies when less. Hence, as has often been said, it is impossible for one drug to suit all bodies; rather, the drug must be intermediate, as being fitted to the intermediate temperament, and must be led in either direction, being judged by its use on each day. For if it appears to have accomplished what is needed, it is proper to continue with it; but if it falls somewhat short, to increase its power, just as also to reduce it, whenever it has acted beyond what is proper.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σαρκοῦται, καθαιρεῖν

By stronger bodies I mean 468 those that are drier in their mixtures, such as are the bodies of farmers, sailors and hunters; by weaker, those that are softer by nature or by habit, such as are the bodies of women, eunuchs and children, and of all who are moist by nature in their mixtures, having a white and soft body. Hard bodies, then, fill with flesh when more verdigris is mixed into the cerate, soft bodies when less. Hence, as has often been said, it is impossible for one drug to suit all bodies. Rather, the drug must be intermediate, as being adapted to the intermediate mixture, and must be brought towards either side, being judged by its use from day to day. For if it appears to have done what is required, one should persist with it; but if it has acted rather deficiently, one should increase its potency, just as one should also reduce it whenever it has acted beyond what is fitting.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐμμέσου, φρυκτῆς

For example, in the case of the drug now before us, the one made from cerate and verdigris, one must consider whether the ulcer has been filled with flesh, or whether flesh was not generated but the ulcer was found foul and full of moisture, or whether, in addition to its not having been filled with flesh, 469 some sensation of biting arose in the patient and the ulcerated part appeared hotter, or at any rate redder. If, then, it appears weaker, you will increase its power by the admixture of honey or verdigris; but if stronger, you will mix in olive oil or rose-oil, softening the drug that is to be taken up on the lint pledgets. On an occasion of need, then, cerate and verdigris are mixed in this way, just as I said they were once mixed by me. But let those who practise medicine have in readiness a prepared plaster, the one they name 'green' from its colour, which is sufficient also to treat fresh wounds, those that are not large, and which can also be melted with rose-oil to the consistency of a drug of intermediate (ἐμμέσου) character. It will also need to contain some resin, either the one called Colophonian and 'roasted' (φρυκτῆς), or terebinth. It has been said that the resin called larch is somewhat similar to terebinth. Here too the quantity in which they are mixed is a matter of conjecture.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στακτῷ, ἐχέκολλον

For if we mix in more resin than is appropriate, while it is being softened by the hands it appears soft, but once spread on a linen cloth it will a little later be hard like leather, 470 easily falling off the body. But if less resin than the due proportion is mixed with the wax, it will not have that quality which is called echekollon ("glue-holding"), so as to be stretched further when it is drawn out; and it is evident that even if it is applied to some part, it will adhere poorly and quickly fall off it. What, then, is the well-proportioned mixture, with a view to a plaster-like consistency? If you would be persuaded by me, an equal amount of each. And let neither the wax be dry and old, nor the resin. For in this way the consistency of what is mixed from them becomes best, if both are taken fatty and soft. But if one of the two should exceed, it is always better for the resin to predominate, so that some even mix in a double quantity with the wax when it is melted (staktos). [Preparation of the simple green plaster of Andromachus, and an exposition concerning its power.] And the green plaster—for so they themselves call it—some have made from a double quantity of the resin, putting in two litrai of it, one of wax, and two kyathoi of oil.

For if we mix in more of the resin than is fitting, it appears soft while it is being softened by the hands, but when spread on linen cloth it will a little later become like hard skin, 470 readily falling off the body. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Of verdigris, some add two, others three ounces to such a mixture 471 of wax and resin. But also in the preparation, some work the verdigris into the meltable ingredients once they have been melted, while others, having cooled them and then scraped them, make the mixture in the mortar. Those, however, who wish to make the drug more agglutinative of bloody wounds first triturate the verdigris in a mortar with vinegar and add it while scraping in the cerate. If, then, anyone asks you which of these green plasters is best, remember what is often said by me, and answer that all are best for the one who knows the conditions to which each is suited, just as all are also bad for the one who does not know. For my part, it is for me to say that the one which takes in vinegar in addition dries more vigorously. And it is quite evident that the one containing more verdigris is both strong and more drying and more cleansing of foul ulcers. The limit of it is that it should effect these things without biting.

Of the verdigris, some add two ounces and others three to such a mixture 471 of the wax and the resin. Moreover, in the preparation some knead the verdigris into the meltable ingredients once these have melted, while others first let them cool, then scrape them off and make the mixture in the mortar. Those who wish to make the drug more agglutinative of bloody wounds first grind the verdigris with vinegar in the mortar and then add the cerate, scraping it in. So if any of you is asked which of these green plasters is best, remember what I have often said, and answer that all are best for one who knows the conditions to which each is suited, just as all are bad for one who does not know them. For my part I say that the one that takes vinegar in addition dries more vigorously. And it is quite clear that the one containing more verdigris is strong, more drying, and more cleansing of foul wounds. Its limit is that it accomplish these things without biting.

τὸ γὰρ οὕτως ἰσχυρὸν, ὡς ἤδη καὶ δάκνειν ἐπιτιθέμενον ἕλκεσι καθαροῖς, οὔτε σαρκώσει ποτ’ αὐτὰ καὶ καθ’ ἕτερον τρόπον ὑγρὰ καὶ ῥυπαρὰ ποιήσει, παραπλησίως τοῖς ἐνδεῶς ξηραίνουσί τε καὶ ῥύπτουσιν, ἀλλ’ ἐπὶ τῶν δριμυτέρων κοιλότερόν γε γίνεται τὸ ἕλκος, ἐρυθρότερόν 472 τε καὶ θερμότερον. ἔστι δ’ ὅτε καὶ αὐτὸς ὁ κάμνων αἰσθάνεται τῆς δήξεως αὐτοῦ, μὴ τοίνυν ἁπλῶς ἐπαινεῖτε φάρμακόν τι χωρὶς τοῦ προσθεῖναι τὴν διάθεσιν, ἐφ’ ἧς ἐπαινεῖται, μήτ’ ἐπ’ ἄλλου τινὸς φαρμάκου μήτε ἐπὶ τῶν νῦν προκειμένων. ὅσοι μὲν οὖν ἀλλήλων ἐφεξῆς τὰς χλωρὰς γράψαντες οὐκ ὤκνησαν διορίσαι τὰς διαθέσεις, ἐφ’ ὧν ἁρμόττουσιν, ἧττον ἁμαρτάνουσι τῶν μηδὲ τοῦτο ποιησάντων. ὅσοι δ’ ἄνευ διορισμῶν γεγράφασι κατὰ τὰς μεταβάσεις αὐτῶν προστάττοντες, ἄλλη χλωρὰ, μοχθηροτάτην ποιοῦνται διδασκαλίαν, εἴ γε δὴ χρὴ διδασκαλίαν ὀνομάζειν ὅλως τὴν τοιαύτην γραφήν. ὁ γοῦν Ἀνδρόμαχος ἐν τῇ βίβλῳ, ἣν τῶν ἐκτὸς ἐπιγράφει, μετ’ ὀλίγα τῆς ἀρχῆς πρώτας μὲν φαιὰς ἔγραψεν, εἶτα πυῤῥὰς, καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα μελαίνας, εἶτα ἐπὶ τὰς χλωρὰς τραπόμενος ἀρχὴν τῆς διδασκαλίας ἐποιήσατο τοιάνδε, προγράψας, χλωραὶ, πρώτην ἁπασῶν ἔγραψε κατὰ λέξεν τήνδε. χλωρὰ Γάλλου. ♃ ῥητίνης στροβιλίνης δραχ. τ΄. κηροῦ δραχ. ρ΄. ἰοῦ ξυστοῦ δραχ. ν΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οὔτε σαρκώσει ποτ’ αὐτὰ καὶ καθ’ ἕτερον τρόπον, ἐν τῇ βίβλῳ, ἣν τῶν ἐκτὸς ἐπιγράφει

For what is so strong that it already even bites when applied to clean ulcers will never make them grow flesh, and will, in a different manner, render them moist and foul, in much the same way as those [drugs] that dry and cleanse insufficiently; but in the case of the more acrid ones the ulcer becomes hollower, redder 472 and hotter. And there are times when the patient himself also perceives its bitingness. Do not, then, simply praise some drug without adding the condition for which it is praised, neither in the case of any other drug nor in the case of those now before us. Those, therefore, who, in writing up the green plasters one after another, did not shrink from defining the conditions for which they are suitable, err less than those who did not even do this. But those who have written without distinctions, prescribing at their transitions, 'another green one', produce a most faulty teaching—if indeed one ought to call such writing teaching at all. Andromachus, at any rate, in the book which he entitles 'Of External Remedies', a little after the beginning wrote first the grey [plasters], then the red, and after these the black; then, turning to the green ones, he made the beginning of his teaching as follows: having written as heading 'Green ones', he wrote the first of them all in these very words. Green plaster of Gallus. Rx: pine-cone resin, 300 drachmas; wax, 100 drachmas; scraped verdigris, 50 drachmas.

For a drug so strong that it already actually bites when applied to clean ulcers will never make them fill with flesh, and in another way it will make them moist and foul, much like those that dry and cleanse insufficiently; but with the more acrid ones the ulcer becomes hollower, and redder 472 and hotter. And sometimes the patient himself perceives its biting. Do not, therefore, praise any drug simply, without adding the condition for which it is praised, neither in the case of any other drug nor in the case of those now before us. Those, then, who wrote out the green [plasters] one after another and did not shrink from distinguishing the conditions for which they are suited err less than those who did not do even this. But those who have written without distinctions, prescribing at the transitions between them 'another green', produce the very worst teaching, if indeed one ought to call such writing teaching at all. Andromachus, at any rate, in the book which he entitles 'Of the external [remedies]', a little after the beginning wrote first the grey ones, then the tawny ones, and after these the black ones; then, turning to the green ones, he made the beginning of the teaching as follows: having written 'Green ones' as a heading, he wrote as the first of all this one, word for word: Green of Gallus. ♃ Of pine resin, 300 drachms; of wax, 100 drachms; of scraped verdigris, 50 drachms.

of frankincense 473 25 drachms, of vinegar a sufficient quantity. He ought perhaps, then, to have said something also about the preparation, whether he wishes the frankincense too to be ground fine together with the verdigris while the vinegar is poured on, or only the verdigris. For either way is possible; and in many compositions some have written it the one way, others the other. 474 and a twelfth and a thirteenth and a fourteenth and a fifteenth and a sixteenth, prefixing to each of them only this one word, "another", writing them all as differing from one another in power, yet not all of them suitable for putrefactions and spreading sores and gangrenes, as can be recognised from their material. From which it is also clear that he wrote them together with one another according to similarity of colour, not according to similarity of power. We, then, shall state the power of each of them, taking our starting-point from the nature of the simple drugs. For this will be most useful to those who wish not only to understand in general the method of the composition and use of such drugs, but also to be able, by this means, to improvise and to discern, in the case of things written by the older authors without distinction, of what sort of power they are. So we shall examine the one which Andromachus wrote first, taking up and considering each of the simples of which it is composed. First, then, in it he mentions pine-cone (strobiline) resin.

ἔστι δ’, ὡς ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῆς τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων δυνάμεως ἐλέγετο, θερμαντικωτάτη 475 τε τῶν ῥητινῶν ἡ τοιαύτη, καθάπερ ἡ τερμινθίνη μετριωτάτη πασῶν εἰς ἄμφω. πᾶσαι μὲν γὰρ αἱ ῥητίναι θερμαίνουσί τε καὶ ξηραίνουσιν, ἀλλ’ αἱ μὲν μᾶλλον, αἱ δ’ ἧττον αὐτῶν ταῦτα δρῶσιν. ἰσχυροτάτη μὲν οὖν ἐστιν ἡ στροβιλίνη, μετριωτάτη δ’, ὡς ἔφην, ἡ τερμινθίνη. καὶ μέντοι καὶ ξηραίνει τάχιστα μὲν ἡ στροβιλίνη καὶ τὸ καλούμενον ἰδίᾳ προσηγορίᾳ πιτύϊνον φύσημα, ῥητίνη μὲν ὑπάρχον τῷ γένει καὶ αὐτὸ, ξηραῖνον δὲ καὶ θερμαῖνον, ἀλλὰ τῷ μὲν ξηραίνειν οὐκ ἀπολείπεται τῆς στροβιλίνης, τῷ θερμαίνειν δὲ οὐκ ὀλίγον λείπεται. αὗται μὲν οὖν ἀλιπεῖς εἰσι ξηρανθεῖσαι, διὸ καὶ τὰς εὐαφεῖς ἐμπλάστρους οὐ δύνανται κατασκευάζειν, ὥσπερ αἱ γλίσχραι τε καὶ ὑγραί. μεταξὺ δὲ τοῦ τε πιτυΐνου φυσήματος καὶ τῆς στροβιλίνης ῥητίνης ἔστιν ἡ ἐλατίνη, κατά γε τὸ θερμαίνειν ὑγρὰ μὴν, ἕως πλείονος αὐτὴ διαμένει, καθάπερ καὶ τῆς κολοφωνίας ἡ λιβανίζουσα, καὶ ταύτην γε μόνην ἔνιοι καλοῦσι κολοφωνίαν, ἡδύ τι κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν ἔχουσα, ὥσπερ ἡ ἐλατίνη καὶ θερμαίνουσα συμμέτρως, ὡς ἐκείνη. γεννᾶται δ’ ὀλίγη πάνυ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τίμιος ὑπάρχει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πιτύϊνον φύσημα, λιβανίζουσα, ἀλιπεῖς

This is, as was said in the books on the power of simple drugs, the most heating 475 of the resins, just as the terebinth is the most moderate of all in both respects. For all resins both heat and dry, but some of them do these things more, others less. The strongest, then, is the strobilos resin, and the most moderate, as I said, the terebinth. And indeed the strobilos resin dries most quickly, as does what is called by its own particular name 'pine physema' (πιτύϊνον φύσημα), which is itself also a resin in kind, drying and heating; but while in drying it does not fall short of the strobilos resin, in heating it falls short by not a little. These, then, are without fattiness when they have dried, and for this reason they cannot produce plasters pleasant to the touch, as the viscous and moist ones do. Between the pine physema and the strobilos resin stands the fir resin, at least as regards heating; it however remains moist for a longer time, just as does the frankincense-like (λιβανίζουσα) variety of the Colophonian—and some call this alone Colophonian—having something pleasant in its smell, like the fir resin, and heating moderately, like that one. It is produced in very small quantity, and for this reason it is costly.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀλιπεῖς, φύσημα, κατά γε τὸ θερμαίνειν ὑγρὰ μὴν, εὐαφεῖς, τίμιος

This kind is, as was said in the books On the Power of Simple Drugs, the most heating 475 of the resins, just as the terebinth is the most moderate of them all in both respects. For all resins heat and dry, but some of them do this more, others less. The strongest, then, is the strobiline, and the most moderate, as I said, the terebinth. And indeed the quickest to dry are the strobiline and what is called by a name of its own the pine 'physema' (pityinon physēma). This too is a resin by kind, and it dries and heats: in drying it does not fall short of the strobiline, but in heating it falls short by no small amount. These, then, become free of fat (?) when dried, which is why they cannot be used to make plasters that are pleasant to the touch, as the sticky and moist resins can. Between the pine physema and the strobiline resin, at least as regards heating, stands the fir resin. It is moist, however, and itself remains so for a longer time, just as does the Colophonian resin that smells of frankincense; some indeed call only this one Colophonian. It has something sweet in its smell, like the fir resin, and it heats moderately, as that one does. It is produced in very small quantity, and for this reason it is costly.

Greek (Kühn)

ὑγροτάτη γε μὴν ἁπασῶν ἐστι τῆς 476 λάρικος ὀνομαζομένης ῥητίνης, ἡ ἑτέρα διαφορά. διττὴ γάρ πώς ἐστι καὶ αὕτη, παραπλησία μὲν ἡ ἑτέρα τῇ τερμινθίνῃ κατὰ πάντα, δριμυτέρα δὲ αὐτῆς ἡ ἑτέρα καὶ τερμοτέρα καὶ ὑγροτέρα καὶ κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν ἀηδεστέρα καὶ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν πικροτέρα καὶ θερμοτέρα. ἡ δ’ ὑφισταμένη τοῖς τὴν πευκίνην ῥητίνην περιέχουσι κεραμείοις ὑγρὰ ῥητίνη κατὰ μὲν τὴν σύστασιν καὶ τὴν χρόαν ἀκριβῶς ἔοικε τῇ τερμινθίνῃ, τῇ δ’ ὀσμῇ καὶ τῇ γεύσει διενήνοχεν, ἀηδεστέρα μὲν οὖσα κατὰ τὴν ὀσμὴν, ὥσπερ καὶ δριμυτέρα, καὶ κατὰ τὴν γεῦσιν δακνωδεστέρα· διὸ καὶ κάλλιόν ἐστι τὴν τερμινθίνην μιγνύειν ἅπασι τοῖς μέλλουσι ἕλκη καλῶς ἰάσασθαι φαρμάκοις, ἐπειδὴ πρῶτός τε καὶ ἄριστος ἐν αὐτοῖς ἐστι σκοπὸς ἄδηκτον εἶναι τὸ προσφερόμενον. ἐπὶ τὴν προκειμένην οὖν ἀνέλθωμεν ἔμπλαστρον, εἰς ἣν ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἐμβαλεῖν κελεύει τῆς στροβιλίνης ῥητίνης τὰς δραχ.τ΄. ταῖς ἑκατὸν δραχμαῖς τοῦ κηροῦ μεμιγμέναις, οὔτε τῆς συστάσεως οὔτε τῆς δυνάμεως ἀκριβῶς ἐστοχασμένος ἢ αὐτὸς ἢ ὅ γε συνθεὶς αὐτήν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τερμοτέρα

The most moist of all, however, is the one variety of the resin called 476 larch. For this too is in a way twofold: the one kind is closely similar to the terebinth in all respects, while the other is more pungent than it and τερμοτέρα and more moist, and more unpleasant in smell and more bitter in taste and hotter. The liquid resin that settles at the bottom of the earthenware vessels containing pine resin exactly resembles the terebinth in consistency and colour, but differs in smell and taste, being more unpleasant in smell, as also more pungent, and more biting in taste; wherefore it is also better to mix the terebinth into all the drugs that are to heal ulcers well, since the first and best aim among them is that what is applied be non-biting. Let us return, then, to the plaster before us, into which Andromachus orders that three hundred drachmas of stone-pine resin be put, mixed with the hundred drachmas of wax, neither he himself nor whoever composed it having aimed accurately at either its consistency or its power.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τερμοτέρα (not a Greek word; probably a corruption, perhaps of θερμοτέρα, which recurs at the end of the same sentence; rendered 'hotter'), στροβιλίνης (rendered 'strobiline resin', i.e. resin of the στρόβιλος / pine-cone tree; the exact tree is uncertain), τὰς δραχ.τ΄. (read as 300 drachms)

Most liquid of all, however, is the second variety of the resin called 476 larch resin. For this too is in a way twofold: one kind is closely similar to terebinth in every respect, while the other is more pungent than it, hotter, more liquid, more unpleasant in smell, and more bitter and hotter in taste. The liquid resin that settles at the bottom of the earthenware vessels holding pine resin resembles terebinth exactly in consistency and colour, but differs from it in smell and taste: it is more unpleasant in smell, and also more pungent, and more biting in taste. For this reason it is better to mix terebinth into all drugs that are meant to heal ulcers well, since the first and foremost aim in these is that what is applied should be non-biting. Let us go back, then, to the plaster under discussion. Into it Andromachus directs one to put 300 drachms of strobiline resin mixed with the hundred drachms of wax. In this neither he himself nor, at any rate, the man who composed it aimed precisely at either the consistency or the potency.

Greek (Kühn)

οὔτε γὰρ ἐχέκολλος ἔσται καὶ δριμεῖα γενήσεται, βοήθημα δ’ αὐτῇ πρὸς ἄμφω καλῶς ἐποίησε τὸν λιβανωτὸν μίξας, ἀλλ’ ὡς 477 πρὸς τὸ πλῆθος ἐκείνων ὀλίγον ἔμιξε καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅτι τοῦ ἰοῦ δραχ.ν΄. ἐμβάλλει, μέρος δ’ εἰσὶν ὄγδοον αὗται τῶν ἐκ τοῦ κηροῦ τε καὶ τῆς ῥητίνης ἀθροιζομένων τετρακοσίων. ἀλλὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὐκ ἄν τις μέμψαιτο τῷ σκοπὸν ἔχοντι ξηραντικὸν ἱκανῶς ἐργάζεσθαι φάρμακον, ἐπείτοι τῷ τε σαρκωτικὸν αὐτὸ καὶ τῶν μετρίων τραυμάτων κολλητικὸν ἀποτελέσαι βουληθέντι, αἱ δραχ. ν΄. καλῶς ἂν μιγνύοιντο. καὶ γένοιτ’ ἂν οὕτω πρᾳότερόν τε καὶ σαρκωτικώτερον ἐχεκολλότερόν τε καὶ πολυχρηστότερον. ἐπαινεῖν δὲ χρὴ τὴν τοῦ λιβάνου μίξιν, εἰς σύστασιν ἐμπλαστρώδη βοηθοῦντος· ἀνώδυνός τε γάρ ἐστι καὶ πεπτικός. εἰ μὲν οὖν ἴσον αὐτοῦ σταθμὸν ἐμίξατο τῷ ἰῷ, τουτέστι τὰς δραχ. ν΄. ἐχεκολλότερόν τ’ ἂν, ὡς ἔφην, εἰργάσατο καὶ πρᾳότερον τὸ φάρμακον. ἐπεὶ δ’ εἴκοσι καὶ ε΄. ἔμιξε, τὴν ξηραντικὴν ἰσχὺν τοῦ ἰοῦ φυλάξαι βουληθεὶς ἐπιτήδειον εἴργασται τὴν ἔμπλαστρον, εἴς τε μειζόνων τραυμάτων κόλλησιν, ἑλκῶν τε δυσθεραπεύτων ἴασιν. ὅτι τε τοιοῦτον αὐτὸ γενέσθαι προὔθετο δηλοῖ καὶ ἡ τοῦ ὄξους μίξις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αἱ δραχ. ν΄. καλῶς ἂν μιγνύοιντο, ἐπείτοι

For it will not be adhesive, and it will become acrid; and he rightly provided it with a remedy against both by mixing in frankincense, but, 477 relative to the quantity of those ingredients, he mixed in little, and especially because he puts in fifty drachms of verdigris, and these are an eighth part of the four hundred that are made up from the wax and the resin. But this one would not fault in someone whose aim is to produce a sufficiently drying drug, since for one wishing to make it flesh-producing and agglutinative for moderate wounds, the fifty drachms would be well mixed in. And in this way it would become milder and more flesh-producing, more adhesive and more widely useful. One must praise the admixture of frankincense, which helps towards a plaster-like consistency; for it is both pain-relieving and concoctive. If, then, he had mixed in a weight of it equal to the verdigris, that is, the fifty drachms, he would, as I said, have made the drug both more adhesive and milder. But since he mixed in twenty-five, wishing to preserve the drying strength of the verdigris, he has made the plaster suitable both for the agglutination of larger wounds and for the cure of ulcers that are hard to treat. And that he intended it to become such is shown also by the admixture of vinegar.

For it will not be adhesive, and it will turn out acrid. He did well to provide a remedy for it against both by mixing in frankincense, but 477 relative to the quantity of those ingredients he mixed in little, and especially since he adds 50 dr. of verdigris, and these are an eighth part of the four hundred made up from the wax and the resin. But no one would fault this in one whose aim is to make a sufficiently drying drug; whereas for one who wished to render it flesh-producing and agglutinative of moderate wounds, the 50 dr. would properly be mixed in. And in this way it would become milder and more flesh-producing, more adhesive and of more manifold use. One must praise the admixture of the frankincense, since it helps towards a plaster-like consistency; for it is both anodyne and concoctive. If, then, he had mixed in an equal weight of it to the verdigris, that is, the 50 dr., he would, as I said, have made the drug more adhesive and milder. But since he mixed in twenty-five, wishing to preserve the drying strength of the verdigris, he has made the plaster suitable both for the agglutination of larger wounds and for the healing of ulcers that are hard to treat. That he intended it to be of this kind is shown also by the admixture of the vinegar.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐν ἡμῖν οὖν ἐστι καὶ σφοδρότερον ἐργάζεσθαι καὶ πρᾳότερον ﻿ 478 αὐτὸ, μιγνύουσι ἤτοι πλέον ἢ ἔλαττον ὄξους, ὡσαύτως δὲ δριμύτερον ἢ ἀδηκτότερον. ἐκ μὲν γὰρ τῆς τοῦ δριμυτέρου μίξεως, ὥσπερ καὶ τῆς τοῦ πλέονος, ξηραντικώτερον ἔσται τὸ φάρμακον, κολλητικώτερόν τε τῶν ἐναίμων, οἰνώδους ὄξους μιχθέντος. ἄμεινον δ’ ἐν τῇ συνθέσει τὸν ἰὸν σὺν ὄξει δριμεῖ λεαίνειν ἡμέραις πλείοσιν ἐν ἡλίῳ θερμῷ. τὸ μὲν γὰρ δηκτικὸν αὐτοῦ πραϋνθήσεται, τῆς δυνάμεως δ’ οὐδὲν ἐλαττωθήσεται. τὸν λιβανωτὸν δ’ οὐκ ἀναγκαῖον ἐξ ἀρχῆς μιγνύειν, ἀλλ’ ἀρκεῖ μία πρὸς ταῦτα ἡμέρα. μεμνῆσθαι γὰρ ὑμᾶς νομίζω καὶ τούτου πολλάκις εἰρημένου τῶν μὲν ἰσχυρῶν φαρμάκων, ὁποῖός ἐστιν ὅ τε ἰὸς καὶ τὸ μίσυ καὶ ἡ χαλκῖτις καὶ τὸ σῶρυ καὶ ἡ λεπὶς ἥ τε χάλκανθος ἐν ὄξει λειουμένων ἡμέραις πλείοσι, τὴν μὲν δῆξιν ἀμβλύνεσθαι, τὴν δ’ ἀρετὴν αὐξάνεσθαι. παχυμερέστερα γὰρ φύσει ὄντα τὰ μεταλλικὰ πάντα, μέχρι βάθους τῶν θεραπευομένων σωμάτων οὐκ ἐκτείνει τὰς δυνάμεις.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

It is therefore in our power to make it both more vigorous and milder 478, by mixing in either more or less vinegar, and likewise more acrid or less biting. For from the admixture of the more acrid vinegar, as also of a greater quantity of it, the drug will be more drying, and more agglutinative of bloody wounds, when wine-like vinegar has been mixed in. It is better, in the composition, to grind the verdigris with sharp vinegar for several days in a hot sun; for its biting quality will be mitigated, while nothing of its power will be diminished. The frankincense, however, it is not necessary to mix in from the beginning; rather a single day suffices for this. For I think you remember this too, which has often been said, that in the case of the strong drugs, such as verdigris, misy, chalcitis, sory, scale and chalcanthum, when they are ground in vinegar for several days, the bitingness is blunted while the virtue is increased. For all the metallic drugs, being by nature of coarser parts, do not extend their powers to the depth of the bodies being treated.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐναίμων, οἰνώδους ὄξους μιχθέντος, ἀρκεῖ μία πρὸς ταῦτα ἡμέρα, λεπὶς

It is therefore in our power to make it either more vigorous or milder 478, by mixing in either more or less vinegar, and likewise sharper or less biting vinegar. For from the admixture of sharper vinegar, as also from that of a larger quantity, the drug will be more drying, and more agglutinative of bloody wounds, when a winy vinegar has been mixed in. In the composition it is better to grind the verdigris with sharp vinegar for several days in hot sun. For its biting quality will be softened, while its power will be diminished not at all. The frankincense need not be mixed in from the beginning; one day is enough for it. For I think you remember this too, since it has often been said: when strong drugs, such as verdigris, misy, chalcitis, sory, lepis and chalcanthum, are ground in vinegar for several days, their biting is blunted and their virtue is increased. For all mineral substances, being by nature rather coarse-particled, do not extend their powers into the depth of the bodies under treatment.

Greek (Kühn)

ὅπως οὖν γένηται λεπτομερέστερα τὴν διὰ τῆς τρίψεως καὶ τοῦ ὄξους αὐτοῖς κατεργασίαν χρὴ προστίθεσθαι· διὰ ταύτης γὰρ ἀκριβῶς λεπτουργηθέντα 479 μέχρι τοῦ βάθους καταδύεται. προσέρχεται δέ τι καὶ ἄλλο χρηστὸν ἐν ταῖς τοιαύταις παρασκευαῖς τῶν φαρμάκων, ἀκριβῶς γὰρ κατεργασθέντα διεξέρχεται τοὺς κατὰ τὸ σῶμα πόρους, μηδαμόθεν κατὰ τὴν ὁδοιπορίαν ἰσχόμενα καὶ διὰ τοῦτο, κᾂν ᾖ θερμότερα φύσει, χωρὶς τοῦ δάκνειν ἐνεργεῖ. καὶ τῶν χυμῶν οὖν αὐτῶν κατὰ τὸ σῶμα γεννωμένων οἱ μὲν λεπτοὶ διαπνέονται ῥᾳδίως ἄνευ δήξεως, οἱ παχύτεροι δὲ κατεχόμενοί τε καὶ σφηνούμενοι λυποῦσιν, ὅταν ὦσι δακνώδεις. ὁ δ’ αὐτὸς λόγος ἐστὶ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν καυθέντων μεταλλικῶν φαρμάκων, ἀλλ’ ἐκεῖνα μὲν ἐκ τῆς τοῦ πυρὸς ὁμιλίας διασώζει τι δακνῶδες, ὃ κατὰ τὰς πλύσεις ἀποθέμενα παντάπασι ἄδηκτα γίνεται. τὰ δ’ ἀντὶ τοῦ πυρὸς ὅμοιόν τι καύσει παθοῦντα κατὰ τὴν δι’ ὄξους ἐν ἡλίῳ τρίψιν οὐδὲ τὴν ἐκ τοῦ πυρὸς ἐπικτᾶται θερμότητα. λειούμενά γε μὴν ἐν ὄξει καὶ τὰ καυθέντα πολὺ βελτίω γίνεται. ταῦτά τε οὖν κοινὰ πολλῶν φαρμάκων ἐστὶν ἀναγκαῖα γιγνώσκεσθαι, τό τε τῆς χρήσεως αὐτῶν ὁμοίως, ἄλλο μὲν ἐπ’ ἄλλων παθῶν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄλλο μὲν ἐπ’ ἄλλων παθῶν

In order, then, that they may become more fine-particled, one must add to them the working-up by means of trituration and vinegar; for having been thoroughly refined through this, they sink down 479 as far as the depth. And there accrues also another benefit in such preparations of drugs: for having been thoroughly worked up, they pass through the pores in the body, being held back nowhere on their journey, and for this reason, even if they are hotter by nature, they act without biting. And so too of the humours themselves that are generated in the body, the thin ones are easily dispersed by transpiration without bitingness, whereas the thicker ones, being retained and wedged in, cause distress, when they are biting. The same account holds also for the burnt metallic drugs; but those retain something biting from their contact with the fire, which they lay aside in the washings and become entirely non-biting. Whereas those which, instead of the fire, undergo something similar to burning in the trituration with vinegar in the sun do not acquire in addition even the heat that comes from the fire. Indeed, even the burnt ones, when levigated in vinegar, become much better. These things, then, which are common to many drugs, are necessary to be known, and likewise what concerns their use, which is different in the case of different affections.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: παθοῦντα (anomalous form, taken as παθόντα, 'undergoing'), ἄλλο μὲν ἐπ’ ἄλλων παθῶν (μέν without answering δέ; sentence may be truncated or corrupt)

So, in order that they may become finer-particled, one must apply to them the processing by means of trituration and vinegar; for when they have been thoroughly refined by this, 479 they sink down to the depth. And there accrues also another benefit in such preparations of drugs: for, when thoroughly processed, they pass through the pores throughout the body, being held back nowhere on their passage, and for this reason, even if they are hotter by nature, they act without biting. So too, of the humours themselves that are generated in the body, the thin ones are readily dispersed by transpiration without biting, whereas the thicker ones, being retained and wedged in, cause distress whenever they are biting. The same argument applies also to burnt metallic drugs; but these retain something biting from their contact with the fire, and when they have laid this aside in the washings they become altogether non-biting. Those, however, which instead of fire undergo something similar to burning through trituration with vinegar in the sun do not even acquire the heat that comes from fire. Yet the burnt ones too become much better when ground fine in vinegar. These things, then, being common to many drugs, are necessary to know, and likewise the matter of their use, one thing in some affections, another in others.

and in the case of hollow ones, when, taken up 480 on lint-pledgets (motoi), they are inserted. The plaster before us, at any rate, if you melt it with rose-oil and use it, will be useful on foul and moist ulcers, but biting on those that are free of complicating circumstances, and for this reason it will be necessary to mix with it a moist cerate made from wax and resin, melted either with rose-oil or with olive oil. So too the oil containing salt, to the extent that it has taken a share of it, is more heating and more drying than that which has not received any; while the oil with tender shoots of olive, which they call thalloi, is more astringent.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

The more useful choice is dictated to you by both the form of the ulcer and the nature of the body being treated, the ulcer being treated by its contraries, the ulcerated body by its similars; so that you will also melt with myrtle oil the drug that is going to be applied on lint, whenever the flesh being generated appears to you to be more flabby than is proper 481, and all the more whenever the ulcer is near to cicatrization; just as, whenever some small remnant of inflammation is left in the surrounding parts, it is best to melt the drug with rose oil. And you have learned that the best rose oil is that which has been made from roses alone, many of them steeped for a considerable time in oil prepared without salt. If, then, one keeps these things always ready to hand in memory and is mindful, as I said, of the power of all the simple drugs, he will both compose green plasters of many kinds himself and make the best use of each of those already compounded. At any rate, the plaster set forth up to this point in our discussion, which Andromachus wrote first among the green ones, can be remodelled in many ways: on the one hand by putting in some other resin in place of the strobilos (pine-cone) resin, on the other by increasing the frankincense or putting in along with it one of the dry flesh-producing drugs, such as iris, aristolochia, bitter-vetch meal, and whatever others are of this kind. And indeed one may also vary the composition by mixing in something emollient for the hardened lips of the ulcer.

What is more useful is dictated to you both by the form of the ulcer and by the nature of the body under treatment: the ulcer is treated by contraries, the ulcerated body by similars. Greek (Kühn)

ἐξ ἧς ἐννοίας ἔνιοι μὲν ἀμμωνιακὸν θυμίαμα 482 καὶ χαλβάνην, ἔνιοι δὲ μυελὸν ἐλάφειον ἢ μόσχειον ἢ στέαρ ἔμιξαν, ὥσπερ γε πάλιν ἄλλοι τῶν ἀποκρουστικῶν τι τὰς ἐπιῤῥοὰς ἀναστεῖλαι βουλόμενοι. κατὰ τοῦτο γοῦν οἱ μὲν τὴν σχιστὴν στυπτηρίαν, οἱ δὲ τὴν στρογγύλην, οἱ δὲ τὴν ὀμφακίνην ἐνέβαλον κηκίδα. τὸ λάδανον δὲ ἕτεροι τὰς δυνάμεις ἀμφοτέρας ἔχον ἔμιξαν μαλακτικήν τε καὶ ἀποκρουστικὴν, ὅπως τὰ χείλη μαλάττοιτο καὶ μηδὲν ἐκ τῶν ὑπερκειμένων ἐπιῤῥέοι. καὶ μὴν καὶ ὅσοι τὰ δυσαλθῆ θεραπεῦσαι ἠβουλήθησαν, ὡς πολύχρηστον εἶναι τὸ φάρμακον, ἔμιξαν τοῖς προειρημένοις χρυσόκολλαν ἢ διφρυγὲς ἢ χαλκὸν κεκαυμένον ἢ λεπίδα, τινὲς μὲν χαλκοῦ, τινὲς δὲ στομώματος ἢ σιδήρου. καὶ σχεδὸν ἅπαντα τὰ νῦν εἰρημένα φυλάττει τὴν χρόαν τῶν χλωρῶν ἐμπλάστρων, ἕτερα δὲ τῶν αὐτῶν ὄντα δυνάμεων, εἰ μιχθείη, διαφθείρει. διόπερ οὐδὲ ἐγὼ πάντων ἐμνημόνευσα τῶν καθ’ ἑκάστην δύναμιν, ἀλλὰ μόνων ἐκείνων, ὅσα τὴν χρόαν φυλάξει τῆς συντιθεμένης ἐμπλάστρου.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στομώματος

On the basis of this notion some mixed in ammoniac incense 482 and galbanum, and some stag's marrow or calf's marrow or fat, just as, again, others mixed in one of the repellent drugs, wishing to check the influxes. On this account, at any rate, some put in schist alum, others the round kind, and others omphacine gall-nut. Others mixed in ladanum, which has both powers, the emollient and the repellent, so that the lips might be softened and nothing might flow in from the parts lying above. And indeed all those who also wished to treat ulcers hard to heal, so that the drug should be a polychreston (of many uses), mixed with the aforesaid ingredients chrysocolla or diphryges or burnt copper or scale, some of copper, some of steel (stomoma) or of iron. And nearly all the things just mentioned preserve the colour of the green plasters, whereas others, though of the same powers, destroy it if they are mixed in. This is why I too did not mention all the drugs under each power, but only those which will preserve the colour of the plaster being compounded.

From this line of thought some mixed in ammoniac incense 482 and galbanum, others deer's or calf's marrow or fat, just as, again, others mixed in one of the repellent drugs, wishing to check the influxes. Accordingly some put in split alum, others round alum, others the omphacine gall. For this reason I too have not mentioned all the substances of each property, but only those that will preserve the colour of the plaster being compounded.

For it was proposed not simply to speak about agglutinative or flesh-producing plasters, but with the condition that they be green ones, so that both these just mentioned and chalcanthum 483 and raw chalcitis, when mixed in a small measure, preserve the colour of the drugs that are the subject of the discussion. This account is necessary; and then, having stated the universal method, it is fitting to make the particular [applications] rest with those who have learned it. But such a thing is the task of dialectical men (διαλεκτικῶν), to write the particular [uses] for them in the case of all [the drugs], as Heras and Criton and Asclepiades and many others do. And indeed there is also a third kind of teaching, which we ourselves practise, neither writing the use in the case of all the particular drugs, nor yet entrusting the whole to the universal, common teaching of the method alone, but teaching this first, and after it writing one, or a second, or even a third example for the sake of clarity and of the exercise of the learners.

484 just as, indeed, we are also now demonstrating these things in practice, as we have already done them also in the preceding books. For having discovered, from the power of the ingredients that compose it, to what degree the drug attains in drying and cleansing, from this we shall also discover the condition for which it will be useful. For where there is much moisture and filth in abundance, there one must apply a drug that dries and cleanses to a considerable degree; but where there is the so-called uncomplicated (aperistaton) ulcer, which needs the production of flesh, there the most moderate of the drying and cleansing drugs. And the less and the more in this it is fitting to discover from the nature of the person being treated. For to harder bodies, whether through natural temperament or through age or through region or pursuits, one must apply drugs that dry more, and to moister bodies those that dry less. For if the hollow ulcer is found to be free of inflammation and 485 clean, one should make no innovation; but if it is moister or fouler, one should intensify the drying and cleansing drug; and if it is more hollow than before, one should relax it. Now it is intensified by the admixture of honey, and it is mitigated by that of oil and cerate or of one of the oily substances, such as rose-oil and myrtle-oil and henna oil prepared simply, without the astringent thickening ingredients (stymmata) which people add either for the sake of fragrance or so that the preparations remain unaffected for a longer time. And if one of the lovers of fault-finding should charge us with leading the argument, contrary to what was set out from the beginning, towards the uses of the drugs, when we had promised to speak about their composition, let him consider that a kind of composition also takes place in the course of use, and hold his peace. For the first composition in it is that of the drugs mixed with the cerate, and the second lies in adding rose, myrtle, henna or rush oil, or honey. And of these things enough; we shall now proceed to the record of the plasters which have already given sufficient proof by experience.

ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος 486 ἀξιόλογα φάρμακα τοῖς ἐπισταμένοις χρῆσθαι. τοιούτων δ’ ὄντων αὐτῶν ἐκλέγεσθαι χρὴ τά τ’ ἐξ ἐλαττόνων ἢ εὐποριστοτέρων τῶν ἁπλῶν συγκείμενα καὶ τὰ πολυχρηστότερα καὶ τὰ τοῦ προκειμένου σκοποῦ μάλιστ’ ἐχόμενα. γένοιτο δ’ ἂν ὃ λέγω σαφέστερον ἐπὶ παραδείγματος. ἔστω δὴ τὸ παράδειγμα κοῖλον ἕλκος ἀπερίστατον ἐν ὑγιεινῷ σώματι. τὸ δὲ τοιοῦτον ἕλκος ὑπὸ τῆς καλουμένης λιτῆς χλωρᾶς ἐμπλάστρου θεραπευθήσεται, τακείσης ἅμα ῥοδίνῳ. λιτὴν δὲ καλοῦσι τὴν ἐξ ἰοῦ καὶ κηροῦ καὶ ῥητίνης. ἐγὼ δὲ, ὡς ἔφην, ἑκατέρου μίγνυμι τὸ ἴσον, ἰοῦ δὲ τὸ δέκατον ἢ ιβ΄ ἢ ὄγδοον. ἔστι δὲ δηλονότι δωδέκατον μὲν, εἰ λίτραν κηροῦ καὶ λίτραν ῥητίνης, ἰοῦ δὲ μίξαιμεν οὐγγίας δύο. τὸ δέκατον δὲ, εἰ ταῖς δύο οὐγγίαις τοῦ ἰοῦ μίξαιμεν εἴκοσι συναμφοτέρου, τῆς τε ῥητίνης καὶ τοῦ κηροῦ, τουτέστιν εἰ ἑκατέρου δέκα. καὶ μέντοι κᾂν εἰ τὸ ὄγδοον μέρος ἰοῦ μίξωμεν τῇ διὰ τοῦ κηροῦ καὶ τῆς ῥητίνης κηρωτῇ, καὶ οὕτως εὖ ἕξει. δριμύτερον μὲν γὰρ ἔσται καὶ ῥυπτικώτερον τὸ τοιοῦτο φάρμακον, οὐ μὴν ἄχρηστόν γε. τοῖς γὰρ σκληροῖς σώμασιν ἐπιτηδείως προσαχθήσεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

These, then, are the drugs that Andromachus 486 wrote down, worthy of note for those who know how to use them. Since they are of this sort, one must select those composed of fewer or more easily procured simples, those of more numerous uses (the more polychrēsta), and those that most closely adhere to the aim in hand. What I am saying would become clearer by an example. Let the example, then, be a hollow ulcer, free of complications, in a healthy body. Such an ulcer will be treated by the so-called plain green plaster, melted together with rose-oil. They call "plain" the one made of verdigris, wax and resin. I, as I said, mix an equal amount of each of the two, and of verdigris a tenth or a twelfth or an eighth. It is evidently a twelfth if we mix a pound of wax and a pound of resin, and two ounces of verdigris; a tenth, if to the two ounces of verdigris we mix twenty of both together, the resin and the wax, that is, if ten of each. And indeed, even if we mix an eighth part of verdigris with the cerate made of wax and resin, it will be fine this way too. For such a drug will be more pungent and more detergent, yet not useless; for it will be suitably applied to hard bodies.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀπερίστατον, κᾂν

These, then, are the drugs that Andromachus wrote down, 486 noteworthy drugs for those who know how to use them. But since they are of this kind, one must select from them those composed of fewer or more readily obtainable simples, those that are more polychrest, and those that keep most closely to the aim proposed. What I mean would become clearer with an example. Let the example, then, be a hollow ulcer, uncomplicated, in a healthy body. Such an ulcer will be treated by the so-called simple green plaster, melted together with rose-oil. They call 'simple' the one made of verdigris, wax and resin. And indeed, even if we mix an eighth part of verdigris into the cerate made of wax and resin, that too will be well. For such a drug will be more acrid and more detergent, yet certainly not useless; for it will be suitably applied to hard bodies.

Greek (Kühn)

487 καλλίων δὲ σύνθεσις ἔσται τὸ μὲν ὄγδοον μέρος ἰοῦ μιξάντων τῇ διὰ τοῦ κηροῦ καὶ τῆς ῥητίνης συνθέσει, προλειωσάντων δὲ πλείοσιν ἡμέραις τὸν ἰὸν μετὰ ὄξους δριμυτάτου. κάλλιον δ’ ἂν εἴη, εἰ καὶ λιβανωτοῦ μίξαιμεν ἥμισυ μέρος ἢ κατὰ τὸν ἰόν. ἐλαίου δὲ μίξεις, ὅταν μὲν χωρὶς ὄξους τε καὶ λιβανωτοῦ σκευάζῃς τὸ φάρμακον, ἰσαρίθμους κυάθους ταῖς οὐγγίαις τοῦ ἰοῦ. ὅταν δὲ λιβανωτὸν καὶ ὄξος μιγνύῃς, ἐὰν μὲν ἡ φρυκτὴ λιπαρωτέρα πως ᾖ καὶ μαλακωτέρα καὶ θέρους σκευάζῃς, οὐδὲν μίξεις· ὅταν δὲ σκληρὰ καὶ χειμῶνος, ἕνα κύαθον. ἔνεστι δέ σοι καὶ τῷ κηρῷ τῶν ὑγρῶν τινα μιγνύοντι ῥητινῶν μηδ’ ὅλως ἐλαίου δεηθῆναι. μίξεις δὲ τῆς μὲν ὑγροτάτης τὸ τρίτον τοῦ κατὰ τὸν κηρὸν σταθμοῦ, τῆς δ’ ἧσσον ὑγρᾶς τὸ ἥμισυ. γενήσεται δὲ δηλονότι τοιαύτη τις σύνθεσις. τὸν κηρὸν καὶ τὴν ῥητίνην τήξας καὶ ψύξας μῖξον ἰοῦ μέρει ποτὲ μὲν ὀγδόῳ, ποτὲ δὲ δεκάτῳ, ποτὲ δὲ δωδεκάτῳ, γιγνώσκων ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν σκληρῶν σωμάτων τήν τε πρώτην συμμετρίαν ἁρμόττειν, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν μαλακῶν τὴν τρίτην, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν μέσων τὴν μέσην.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φρυκτὴ

487 But a finer composition will result if one mixes the eighth part of verdigris with the composition made from wax and resin, having first triturated the verdigris for several days with the most acrid vinegar. And it would be finer still if we also mixed in frankincense, in half the amount relative to the verdigris. Of oil you will mix in, whenever you prepare the drug without vinegar and frankincense, cyathi equal in number to the ounces of verdigris; but whenever you mix in frankincense and vinegar, if the phryktē (roasted resin) is somewhat fattier and softer and you are preparing it in summer, you will mix in none; but when it is hard and it is winter, one cyathus. It is also possible for you, by mixing one of the liquid resins with the wax, to have no need of oil at all. You will mix in, of the most liquid resin, a third of the weight of the wax, and of the less liquid, a half. The composition will evidently turn out to be something like this: having melted the wax and the resin and cooled them, mix them with a part of verdigris, sometimes an eighth, sometimes a tenth, sometimes a twelfth, knowing that for hard bodies the first proportion is suitable, for soft ones the third, and for intermediate ones the intermediate.

487 A finer composition will result if one mixes an eighth part of verdigris into the composition made with wax and resin, having first ground the verdigris for several days with the most acrid vinegar. Of oil you will mix in, when you prepare the drug without vinegar and frankincense, as many cyathi as there are ounces of verdigris. But when you mix in frankincense and vinegar, if the roasted [resin] is somewhat fattier and softer and you are preparing it in summer, you will mix in none; but when it is hard, and in winter, one cyathus. It is also possible for you, if you mix one of the liquid resins with the wax, to need no oil at all. Of the most liquid you will mix in a third of the weight of the wax, and of the less liquid one a half. Such a composition, clearly, will come about as follows: having melted and cooled the wax and the resin, mix them with a part of verdigris, sometimes an eighth, sometimes a tenth, sometimes a twelfth, recognising that for hard bodies the first proportion is suitable, for soft ones the third, and for intermediate ones the intermediate.

488 You will triturate the verdigris with vinegar. And if you should wish to make the drug milder, you may add a weight of frankincense half of that which you used for the verdigris. And if someone [wishes it] milder than this, you will mix the verdigris in a quantity equal to the frankincense. And also without scraping, having cooled the resin together with the wax, we melt them and pour them onto the verdigris and the frankincense, which have been triturated in a mortar with vinegar. These, then, are the first compositions of the green plaster, which is agglutinative of bloody wounds, those that are not large, and which is able to heal the others as far as cicatrization and to produce flesh, if it is melted with some oily liquid. By oily I mean rose-oil, which it is most fitting to use, prepared in the manner I have described, and after this myrtle-oil and henna-oil and mastic-oil, and oil itself: in those cases where you wish to add some heat to the drug being melted you will mix in this common oil; where cooling, the so-called omotribes (oil of unripe olives); where astringency, myrtle-oil.

488 You will grind the verdigris with vinegar. If you should wish to make the drug milder, put in a weight of frankincense half of that which you used of the verdigris.
